It’s been happening enough that it’s starting to worry me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these memory issues?\n\nPatient: It’s been really causing problems for about a year and a couple months now. I’d say it started after that heart scare, when I had to cut back on the walking and little projects that used to keep me steadier.\n\nDoctor: Are these memory problems affecting your ability to perform daily tasks or maintain your independence?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is affecting things. I still get by on my own, but people can’t really count on me like they used to. The senior-center repair group, for example, I’ve let them down a few times. And my doctor’s office has had to call after me about missed heart follow-ups. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to concentrate or make decisions?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly with concentrating. I’ll sit down at the bridge table, or at one of the Fix-It Friends planning meetings, and I mean to pay attention. I get frustrated and embarrassed about things, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been feeling sad or depressed lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble learning new things, like a new game or a new skill?\n\nPatient: I can usually learn something new if I stay with it, but the hard part is keeping my attention on it. Like at bridge or those Fix-It Friends meetings, I start out really trying to focus, and then after a while my mind just drifts. Then I realize I missed a good chunk of what was said or explained.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep lately?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed any big changes. My sleep seems about the same as usual, as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or nervous lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I get worked up or irritated sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed feeling anxious or nervous lately.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’d say it started after that heart scare, when I had to cut back on the walking and little projects that used to keep me steadier.\n\nDoctor: Are these memory problems affecting your ability to perform daily tasks or maintain your independence?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is affecting things. I still get by on my own, but people can’t really count on me like they used to. The senior-center repair group, for example, I’ve let them down a few times. And my doctor’s office has had to call after me about missed heart follow-ups. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to concentrate or make decisions?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly with concentrating. I’ll sit down at the bridge table, or at one of the Fix-It Friends planning meetings, and I mean to pay attention. But after a bit my mind just kind of drifts off, and then I realize I missed half of what people were saying. It’s frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself feeling disoriented or unsure of the date, time, or your location?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I usually know where I am and what day it is. I might have to think for a second sometimes, but I don’t feel lost or disoriented.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad, empty, or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I get frustrated and embarrassed about things, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been feeling sad or depressed lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble learning new things, like a new game or a new skill?\n\nPatient: I can usually learn something new if I stay with it, but the hard part is keeping my attention on it. Like at bridge or those Fix-It Friends meetings, I start out really trying to focus, and then after a while my mind just drifts. Then I realize I missed a good chunk of what was said or explained.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep lately?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed any big changes. My sleep seems about the same as usual, as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or nervous lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I get worked up or irritated sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed feeling anxious or nervous lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your ability to perform tasks that you were previously able to do with ease?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say so. The repair group at the senior center used to be able to count on me more, and lately that hasn’t been the case. And my doctor’s office has had to call and chase me down about missed heart follow-ups, which didn’t used to happen like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your personality or the way you interact with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a bit. I’ve noticed I jump into other people’s conversations more than I should. Like if folks nearby are talking about a project or even a card hand, I’ll just give my opinion before anyone asks me. Then afterward I realize I kind of barged in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties with finding the right words when speaking or following conversations?\n\nPatient: Not so much finding words, no. It’s more following along. I’ll sit down at the bridge table or in one of our Fix-It Friends planning meetings meaning to pay attention, and after a little while my mind just kind of drifts off. Then I realize I missed half of what people were saying.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

And my doctor’s office has had to call after me about missed heart follow-ups. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to concentrate or make decisions?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly with concentrating. I’ll sit down at the bridge table, or at one of the Fix-It Friends planning meetings, and I mean to pay attention. But after a bit my mind just kind of drifts off, and then I realize I missed half of what people were saying. It’s frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself feeling disoriented or unsure of the date, time, or your location?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I usually know where I am and what day it is. I might have to think for a second sometimes, but I don’t feel lost or disoriented.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad, empty, or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I get frustrated and embarrassed about things, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been feeling sad or depressed lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble learning new things, like a new game or a new skill?\n\nPatient: I can usually learn something new if I stay with it, but the hard part is keeping my attention on it. Like at bridge or those Fix-It Friends meetings, I start out really trying to focus, and then after a while my mind just drifts. Then I realize I missed a good chunk of what was said or explained.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep lately?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed any big changes. My sleep seems about the same as usual, as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or nervous lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I get worked up or irritated sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed feeling anxious or nervous lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your ability to perform tasks that you were previously able to do with ease?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say so. The repair group at the senior center used to be able to count on me more, and lately that hasn’t been the case. And my doctor’s office has had to call and chase me down about missed heart follow-ups, which didn’t used to happen like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your personality or the way you interact with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a bit. I’ve noticed I jump into other people’s conversations more than I should. Like if folks nearby are talking about a project or even a card hand, I’ll just give my opinion before anyone asks me. Then afterward I realize I kind of barged in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties with finding the right words when speaking or following conversations?\n\nPatient: Not so much finding words, no. It’s more following along. I’ll sit down at the bridge table or in one of our Fix-It Friends planning meetings meaning to pay attention, and after a little while my mind just kind of drifts off. Then I realize I missed half of what people were saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding written or spoken language?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I can understand what people are saying, and reading makes sense to me. It’s more that I might not stay with it the whole time, but the words themselves aren’t hard to understand.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I still like the things I’ve always liked. I haven’t really noticed losing interest in them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your coordination or balance lately?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t really noticed anything like that. I feel about the same getting around—no new balance problems or coordination changes that I can think of.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

And my doctor’s office has had to call and chase me down about missed heart follow-ups, which didn’t used to happen like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your personality or the way you interact with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a bit. I’ve noticed I jump into other people’s conversations more than I should. Like if folks nearby are talking about a project or even a card hand, I’ll just give my opinion before anyone asks me. Then afterward I realize I kind of barged in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties with finding the right words when speaking or following conversations?\n\nPatient: Not so much finding words, no. It’s more following along. I’ll sit down at the bridge table or in one of our Fix-It Friends planning meetings meaning to pay attention, and after a little while my mind just kind of drifts off. Then I realize I missed half of what people were saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding written or spoken language?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I can understand what people are saying, and reading makes sense to me. It’s more that I might not stay with it the whole time, but the words themselves aren’t hard to understand.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I still like the things I’ve always liked. I haven’t really noticed losing interest in them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your coordination or balance lately?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t really noticed anything like that. I feel about the same getting around—no new balance problems or coordination changes that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling less motivated to do things than you used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that. I still get up and do the things I need to do, and I don’t feel like my motivation has really dropped off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Like heart follow-up appointments, or I forget to call the clinic back. Sometimes I’ll go to do a repair job and realize I didn’t bring the right tools. It’s been happening enough that it’s starting to worry me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these memory issues?\n\nPatient: It’s been really causing problems for about a year and a couple months now. I’d say it started after that heart scare, when I had to cut back on the walking and little projects that used to keep me steadier.\n\nDoctor: Are these memory problems affecting your ability to perform daily tasks or maintain your independence?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is affecting things. I still get by on my own, but people can’t really count on me like they used to. The senior-center repair group, for example, I’ve let them down a few times. And my doctor’s office has had to call after me about missed heart follow-ups. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to concentrate or make decisions?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly with concentrating. I’ll sit down at the bridge table, or at one of the Fix-It Friends planning meetings, and I mean to pay attention. It’s frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself feeling disoriented or unsure of the date, time, or your location?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I usually know where I am and what day it is. I might have to think for a second sometimes, but I don’t feel lost or disoriented.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad, empty, or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I get frustrated and embarrassed about things, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been feeling sad or depressed lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble learning new things, like a new game or a new skill?\n\nPatient: I can usually learn something new if I stay with it, but the hard part is keeping my attention on it. Like at bridge or those Fix-It Friends meetings, I start out really trying to focus, and then after a while my mind just drifts. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to concentrate or make decisions?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly with concentrating. I’ll sit down at the bridge table, or at one of the Fix-It Friends planning meetings, and I mean to pay attention. But after a bit my mind just kind of drifts off, and then I realize I missed half of what people were saying. It’s frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself feeling disoriented or unsure of the date, time, or your location?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I usually know where I am and what day it is. I might have to think for a second sometimes, but I don’t feel lost or disoriented.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad, empty, or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I get frustrated and embarrassed about things, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been feeling sad or depressed lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble learning new things, like a new game or a new skill?\n\nPatient: I can usually learn something new if I stay with it, but the hard part is keeping my attention on it. Like at bridge or those Fix-It Friends meetings, I start out really trying to focus, and then after a while my mind just drifts. Then I realize I missed a good chunk of what was said or explained.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep lately?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed any big changes. My sleep seems about the same as usual, as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or nervous lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I get worked up or irritated sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed feeling anxious or nervous lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your ability to perform tasks that you were previously able to do with ease?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say so. The repair group at the senior center used to be able to count on me more, and lately that hasn’t been the case. And my doctor’s office has had to call and chase me down about missed heart follow-ups, which didn’t used to happen like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your personality or the way you interact with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a bit. I’ve noticed I jump into other people’s conversations more than I should. Like if folks nearby are talking about a project or even a card hand, I’ll just give my opinion before anyone asks me. Then afterward I realize I kind of barged in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties with finding the right words when speaking or following conversations?\n\nPatient: Not so much finding words, no. It’s more following along. I’ll sit down at the bridge table or in one of our Fix-It Friends planning meetings meaning to pay attention, and after a little while my mind just kind of drifts off. Then I realize I missed half of what people were saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding written or spoken language?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I can understand what people are saying, and reading makes sense to me. It’s more that I might not stay with it the whole time, but the words themselves aren’t hard to understand.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I still like the things I’ve always liked. I haven’t really noticed losing interest in them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your coordination or balance lately?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t really noticed anything like that. I feel about the same getting around—no new balance problems or coordination changes that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling less motivated to do things than you used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that. I still get up and do the things I need to do, and I don’t feel like my motivation has really dropped off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen or heard things that aren't really there?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t seen or heard things that weren’t really there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any major medical conditions or surgeries, aside from the heart scare you mentioned, that could be affecting your memory or concentration?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can think of. I haven’t had any other major medical problems or surgeries that I know would be tied to my memory or concentration.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your memory and concentration problems are getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure they’re getting worse.
